**Egypt**,[^cite_note-25] officially the **Arab Republic of Egypt**, is a country spanning the [northeast corner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Africa) of Africa and southwest corner of Asia via the [Sinai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula). It is bordered by the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) to [the north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_coast_of_Egypt), [Palestine](241405.md) and [Israel](9282173.md) to [the northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt%E2%80%93Israel_barrier), the [Red Sea](26191.md) to the east, [Sudan](27421.md) and the [Sahara](325363.md) to [the south](62951120.md), and [Libya](17633.md) to [the west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt%E2%80%93Libya_border). The [Gulf of Aqaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Aqaba) in the northeast separates Egypt from [Jordan](7515964.md) and [Saudi Arabia](349303.md). [Cairo](6293.md) is the capital, [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Egypt), and leading cultural centre, while [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria) is the second-largest city and an important hub of industry and tourism.[^cite_note-gov-26] With over 107 million inhabitants, Egypt is [the most populous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Arab_League_countries_by_population) country in the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world), [third-most populous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_population) country in [Africa](5334607.md), and [15th-most populated in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population).&#x20;

Egypt has one of the longest histories of any country, tracing its heritage along the [Nile Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile_Delta) back to the 6th–4th millennia BCE. Considered a [cradle of civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cradle_of_civilisation), [Ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt) saw some of the earliest developments of writing, agriculture, urbanisation, organised religion and central government.[^cite_note-27] Following nearly three millennia of [pharaonic](23294.md) rule, Egypt came under foreign rule beginning in the 6th century BC. Egypt was an early and important [centre of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Egypt), later [adopting Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization_of_Egypt) from the seventh century onwards. [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), Egypt's former capital and currently second largest city, was a hub of global knowledge through its [Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Alexandria). Cairo became the capital of the [Fatimid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate) in the tenth century and of the subsequent [Mamluk Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate) in the 13th century. Egypt then became part of the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) in 1517, until its local ruler [Muhammad Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_of_Egypt) established modern Egypt as an autonomous [Khedivate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khedivate_of_Egypt) in 1867. The country was then [occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Egypt_under_the_British) by the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) along with [Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Sudan) and gained independence in 1922 as [a monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_dynasty).&#x20;

After the [1948 Arab–Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_War), Egypt [occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic_occupation_of_the_Gaza_Strip) the Gaza Strip. Following the [1952 revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_Egyptian_revolution), Egypt declared itself a [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Egypt_\(1953%E2%80%9358\)). In 1958, Egypt [merged with Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Syrian_Republic) to form the [United Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic), with Syria withdrawing in 1961 after their [coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat). Egypt fought [several armed conflicts](7960202.md) with Israel in 1948, [1956](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis), [1967](29328.md) and [1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War). After the 1967 war, Egypt lost the Sinai Peninsula and the Gaza Strip to Israel. In 1978, Egypt signed the [Camp David Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_David_Accords), which [recognised Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt%E2%80%93Israel_peace_treaty) and heavily demilitarised the Sinai in exchange for the latter's withdrawal from the occupied Sinai, completely turning over control of the Sinai to Egypt in 1982. After the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring), which led to the [2011 Egyptian revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Egyptian_revolution) and overthrow of [Hosni Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosni_Mubarak), the country faced a [protracted period of political unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Crisis_\(2011%E2%80%932014\)); its first [democratic election in 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Egyptian_presidential_election) resulted in the short-lived, [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md)-aligned government of [Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi), which was [overthrown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Egyptian_coup_d%27etat) by the military after [mass protests in 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_2013_Egyptian_protests). The current government is a [semi-presidential republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential_republic) led by [Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md), who was [elected in 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Egyptian_presidential_election) but is widely regarded as [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian).[^cite_note-dictator-28]&#x20;

Egypt is a [developing country](78449.md) with the [second-largest economy in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)). It is considered to be a [regional power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power) in the Middle East, North Africa and the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world), and a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) worldwide. [Islam](6037917.md) is the [official religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion) and [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) its official language. Egypt is a founding member of the [United Nations](31769.md), the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement), the [Arab League](52625.md), the [African Union](10634933.md), [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md), [World Youth Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Youth_Forum), and a member of [BRICS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRICS).[^cite_note-CoARE-1][^cite_note-29][^cite_note-IMFOCT2024-30]&#x20;

## Names

[Ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt) had several names; one of them was ([𓆎](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Special\:Search/%F0%93%86%8E) [𓅓](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Special\:Search/%F0%93%85%93) [𓏏](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Special\:Search/%F0%93%8F%8F)𓊖) *km.t*, which is formed from [𓆎 𓅓](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/km#Egyptian), meaning black.[^cite_note-31] This likely refers to the [fertile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_fertility) black soils of the [Nile floodplains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile_flood), distinct from the *deshret* ⟨*dšṛt*⟩, or 'red land' of the [desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert).[^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33] This name is commonly vocalised as [Kemet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Km_and_Km.t_\(Kemet\)_\(hieroglyphs\)) \[kɛmɛt], but was pronounced differently in Ancient Egyptian.[^cite_note-34] Scholars reconstruct its [Old Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Egyptian) pronunciation as \[ˈkuːmat],[^cite_note-35] its [Middle Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Egyptian) pronunciation as \[ˈkuːmaʔ], and its [Late Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Egyptian) pronunication as \[ˈkeːmə].[^cite_note-36] The name is realised as *K(h)ēmə* ([Bohairic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_language#Dialects): ⲭⲏⲙⲓ, [Sahidic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_language#Dialects): ⲕⲏⲙⲉ) in [Egyptian Coptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_language), and appeared in [Early Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) as Χημία (*_Khēmía_*).[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38] Another name was ⟨*tꜣ-mry*⟩ 'land of the riverbank'.[^cite_note-39]&#x20;

The names of [Upper and Lower Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_and_Lower_Egypt) were *Ta-Sheme'aw* (⟨*tꜣ-šmꜥw*⟩) '[sedgeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedge)' and *Ta-Mehew* (⟨*tꜣ mḥw*⟩) 'northland', respectively. They were also collectively called *tꜣwy* (*tāwy*), meaning '[The Two Lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_and_Lower_Egypt#Structure)', referring to both Lower Egypt and Upper Egypt after unification.[^cite_note-TaWy-40]&#x20;

The English name *Egypt* is derived from the [Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek) *Aígyptos* (Αἴγυπτος), via [Middle French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_French) *Egypte* and [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) *Aegyptus*. It is reflected in early Greek [Linear B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linear_B) tablets as *a-ku-pi-ti-yo*.[^cite_note-41] The [ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) [geographer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographer) [Strabo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strabo) provided a [folk etymology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_etymology) stating that Αἴγυπτος (*Aigýptios*) had originally evolved as a [compound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compound_\(linguistics\)) from Aἰγαίου ὑπτίως (*Aegaeou huptiōs*), meaning '[below the Aegean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea)'.[^cite_note-Engsheden-42] Another tradition claims that it was named after the legendary king [Aegyptus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegyptus).[^cite_note-Jerome-Chronicon-43] The actual derivation is thought to be from '*[ḥwt-kꜣ-ptḥ](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E1%B8%A5wt-k%EA%9C%A3-pt%E1%B8%A5#Egyptian)* (*Hutkaptah*, [Late Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Egyptian) pronunciation [\[ħəjˌkojpəˈtaħ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA)), '[temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_temple) of the *[Ka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egyptian_conception_of_the_soul#Ka_\(vital_essence\))* of [Ptah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptah)," a term for the city of [Memphis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis,_Egypt).[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45]&#x20;

Greco-Roman writers report that Egypt was sometimes called *Aeria* (Ἀερία).[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-Jerome-Chronicon-43][^cite_note-Stephanus_of_Byzantium-Aeria-47][^cite_note-George_W\._Mooney-48] [Stephanus of Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephanus_of_Byzantium) derives the name from *aer* (ἀήρ, 'air'), explaining that Egypt was a "misty land". From this name, the ethnic form *Aerioi* (Ἀέριοι) is also recorded for its inhabitants.[^cite_note-Stephanus_of_Byzantium-Aeria-47] [Thomas George Tucker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_George_Tucker) suggested that *Aeria* could derive not only from "misty" or "hazy air", but also be understood in the sense of "far-off" or "dimly seen".[^cite_note-George_W\._Mooney-48]&#x20;

*Miṣr* (Arabic pronunciation: [\[misˤɾ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Arabic); مِصر) is the [Classical Quranic Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Arabic) and modern official name of Egypt, while *Maṣr* (Egyptian Arabic pronunciation: [\[mɑsˤɾ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Egyptian_Arabic); مَصر) is the local pronunciation in [Egyptian Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Arabic).[^cite_note-49] The current name of Egypt, *Misr*/*Misir*/*Misru*, stems from the [Ancient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Semitic-speaking_peoples) [Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_languages) name for it. The term originally connoted "[Civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civilization)" or "[Metropolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolis)".[^cite_note-AncientCivils-50] [Classical Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Arabic) *Miṣr* ([Egyptian Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Arabic_language): *Maṣr*) is directly cognate with the [Biblical Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_Hebrew_language) *Miṣráyīm* (מִצְרַיִם / מִצְרָיִם), meaning 'the two straits', a reference to the predynastic separation of [Upper and Lower Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_and_Lower_Egypt). Also mentioned in several Semitic languages as *Mesru*, *Misir* and *Masar*.[^cite_note-AncientCivils-50] The oldest attestation of this name for Egypt is the [Akkadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_language) *mi-iṣ-ru* (*miṣru*)[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] related to *miṣru*/*miṣirru*/*miṣaru*, meaning 'border' or 'frontier'.[^cite_note-akkadian-53] The [Neo-Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Assyrian_Empire) used the derived term , *Mu-ṣur*.[^cite_note-54]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Egypt)

### Prehistoric Egypt

Main article: [Prehistoric Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Egypt)



**Clockwise**: a [Badarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badarian_culture) mortuary figurine, a [Naqada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqada_culture) jar, a [Naqada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqada_culture) statuette of the goddess [Bat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bat_\(goddess\)), the [Four dogs palette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmetic_palette), the [Gebel el-Arak Knife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gebel_el-Arak_Knife), and a [Naqada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqada_culture) diorite vase.

Evidence of [rock carvings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_carvings) along the [Nile](21244.md) and in surrounding oases indicates early habitation. In the [10th millennium BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/10th_millennium_BCE), a culture of [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer) and [fishers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing) was replaced by a [grain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cereal)-grinding culture. Climate changes or [overgrazing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overgrazing) around 8000 [BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Before_common_era) began to desiccate the pastoral lands of Egypt, forming the [Sahara](325363.md). Early [tribal peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribal_people) migrated to the Nile River where they developed a settled agricultural [economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_system) and more centralised society.[^cite_note-55]&#x20;

By about 6000 BCE, a [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) culture took root in the Nile Valley.[^cite_note-56] During the Neolithic era, several predynastic cultures developed independently in [Upper and Lower Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_and_Lower_Egypt). The [Badarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badarian) culture and the successor [Naqada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqada) series are generally regarded as precursors to [dynastic Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt). The earliest known Lower Egyptian site, Merimda, predates the Badarian by about seven hundred years. Contemporaneous Lower Egyptian communities coexisted with their southern counterparts for more than two thousand years, remaining culturally distinct, but maintaining frequent contact through trade. The earliest known evidence of [Egyptian hieroglyphic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_hieroglyphs) inscriptions appeared during the predynastic period on Naqada III pottery vessels, dated to about 3200 BCE.[^cite_note-57]&#x20;

### Ancient Egypt (3150 BCE–305 BCE)

Main article: [Ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt)



The [Giza Necropolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giza_Necropolis) is the oldest of the [ancient Wonders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Wonders_of_the_Ancient_World) and the only one still in existence.

Around c. 3150 BCE, King [Menes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menes) unified Egypt, establishing a succession of [dynasties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Egyptian_dynasties) that ruled for three millennia. [Egyptian civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Egypt) thrived with distinctive achievements in [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egyptian_religion), [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_Ancient_Egypt), and [writing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_language). The [Old Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Kingdom) (c. 2700–2200 BCE) saw the construction of the [pyramids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_pyramids), including those at [Giza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giza_Necropolis). A brief [interregnum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intermediate_Period_of_Egypt) followed, succeeded by the [Middle Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Kingdom_of_Egypt) (c. 2040 BCE), a phase of renewed stability and prosperity under rulers such as [Amenemhat III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amenemhat_III).[^cite_note-58]&#x20;

After the [Second Intermediate Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Intermediate_Period_of_Egypt) and the [Hyksos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyksos) occupation, Egypt was reunified by [Ahmose I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmose_I), founder of the [Eighteenth Dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighteenth_dynasty_of_Egypt) and the [New Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Kingdom_of_Egypt) (c. 1550–1070 BCE). This era marked Egypt's height as a major power in the region, extending influence into [Nubia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nubia) and the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant). It produced many of Egypt's most renowned [Pharaohs](23294.md), [Hatshepsut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hatshepsut), [Thutmose III](155730.md), [Akhenaten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhenaten), [Tutankhamun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tutankhamun), and [Ramesses II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramesses_II), and witnessed the rise of [Atenism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atenism), one of the earliest forms of [monotheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism). Despite later invasions by [Libyans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Libya), [Nubians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kush), and [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria), native dynasties eventually reasserted control.[^cite_note-59]&#x20;



The Weighing of the Heart from the *[Book of the Dead of Ani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papyrus_of_Ani)*

In 525 BCE, [Cambyses II of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambyses_II_of_Persia) conquered Egypt, beginning the [Achaemenid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire) [satrapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satrapy) period (the [Twenty-seventh Dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-seventh_Dynasty_of_Egypt)). Although several revolts occurred, Egypt remained under Persian control until briefly regaining independence before falling again in 343 BCE.[^cite_note-60] The [Thirtieth Dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirtieth_dynasty_of_Egypt) was the last native royal house. Following renewed Persian domination, [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) conquered Egypt in 332 BCE, after which his general [Ptolemy I Soter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy_I_Soter) established the [Ptolemaic dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_dynasty).[^cite_note-61]&#x20;

### Ptolemaic and Roman Egypt (305 BCE–641 CE)

Main articles: [Ptolemaic Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_Kingdom) and [Roman Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Egypt)



The Ptolemaic Queen [Cleopatra VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleopatra_VII) and her son by Julius Caesar, [Caesarion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesarion), at the [Temple of Dendera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dendera_Temple_complex)

The [Ptolemaic Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_Kingdom) was a [Hellenistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_civilization) state from southern Syria to [Cyrene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrene,_Libya) and south to Nubia, with [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria) as its capital and a centre of [Greek](42056.md) culture and trade. The Ptolemies adopted pharaonic traditions to legitimise their rule, appearing on monuments in Egyptian style and participating in local religious life.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63] The [Lighthouse of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lighthouse_of_Alexandria), built c. 280 BCE, was one of the [Seven Wonders of the Ancient World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Wonders_of_the_Ancient_World), later destroyed by earthquakes. The last ruler, [Cleopatra VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleopatra_VII), committed suicide after [Octavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octavian) captured Alexandria, ending the dynasty and paving the way for Roman annexation. Native rebellions and dynastic disputes weakened the kingdom, facilitating its annexation by Rome.&#x20;

Egypt was a wealthy imperial province of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), supplying grain and hosting the major city of Alexandria.[^cite_note-64] Governed with Roman administration and Hellenistic culture, its population primarily spoke [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek) in major cities and [Coptic Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_language) in rural areas.[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-pop-66] Christianity reached Egypt in the 1st century, brought by [Saint Mark the Evangelist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Mark_the_Evangelist).[^cite_note-georgetown-67] During [Diocletian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocletian)'s reign (284–305 CE), the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament) had been translated into Egyptian and many Egyptian Christians were persecuted. By CE 451, a distinct [Coptic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria) was firmly established.[^cite_note-68]&#x20;

### Medieval Egypt (641–1517)

Main article: [Egypt in the Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_in_the_Middle_Ages)



The [Amr ibn al-As](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amr_ibn_al-As) mosque in Cairo, recognised as the oldest in Africa

The Ibn Tulun Mosque in Cairo, of Ahmad Ibn Tulun

The [Ibn Tulun Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Tulun_Mosque) in Cairo, of [Ahmad Ibn Tulun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Ibn_Tulun)



The [Al-Hakim Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Hakim_Mosque) in Cairo, of [Al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Hakim_bi-Amr_Allah), the sixth [Fatimid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid) caliph

The Byzantines regained control of Egypt after a brief [Sasanid Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire) invasion early in the 7th century, until 639–42, when the country was conquered by Arab Muslim forces under [Amr ibn al-As](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amr_ibn_al-As) during the [Early Muslim conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests). The Arabs defeated the Byzantine armies, bringing [Islam](6037917.md) to Egypt. Alexandria briefly returned to Byzantine control in 645 but fell again to the Arabs in 646. In 654, an invasion fleet sent by [Constans II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constans_II) was repulsed.[^cite_note-georgetown-67] The Arabs founded [Fustat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fustat), later replaced by Cairo in 969.&#x20;

Under the [Abbasid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid) caliphate, Egypt was governed through deputies residing in Baghdad. Revolts occurred frequently, including the Egyptian revolt of 828 and the uprising of 831 when Copts joined Muslims against the government. Semi-independent dynasties arose, including the [Tulunid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulunid_dynasty) (868–905) and [Ikhshidid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikhshidid_dynasty) (935–969), which maintained Abbasid allegiance while exercising local authority.&#x20;

The [Fatimid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate) ruled Egypt from the 10th century, with [Cairo](6293.md) as their capital. After the Fatimids, the [Ayyubid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayyubid_dynasty) governed until 1250, when the [Mamluks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk), a military caste of [Turco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_people)-[Circassian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians) origin, took control. The Mamluks ruled Egypt for the next three centuries and maintained control over parts of the Levant. By the late 13th century, Egypt linked trade routes connecting the Red Sea with India, Malaya, and the East Indies.[^cite_note-Abu-Lughod-69] The mid-14th century [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death) killed about 40% of Egypt's population.[^cite_note-70]&#x20;

### Ottoman Egypt (1517–1867)

Main articles: [Egypt Eyalet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_Eyalet) and [History of Egypt under the Muhammad Ali dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Egypt_under_the_Muhammad_Ali_dynasty)





[Muhammad Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_of_Egypt), founder of the [Muhammad Ali dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_dynasty), and the extent of their realm.

Egypt was conquered by the [Ottomans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turks) in 1517, following the defeat of the [Mamluk Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate), and became a province of the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md). The Mamluks, who had dominated Egypt's military and political life for centuries, retained influence under Ottoman rule, creating a semi-autonomous power structure. The Ottomans faced repeated challenges in maintaining control, while plagues and famines weakened the economy and civil society. Between 1687 and 1731, Egypt experienced six major famines, including the 1784 famine that killed roughly one-sixth of the population.[^cite_note-Abu-Lughod-69][^cite_note-Icelandic'-71]&#x20;

In 1798, [Napoleon Bonaparte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_Bonaparte) invaded Egypt, defeating the Mamluks at the [Battle of the Pyramids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Pyramids). The French occupation was short-lived, but it destabilised the region and set the stage for Muhammad Ali Pasha's rise. After the French were expelled, power struggles ensued between the Ottomans, the Mamluks, and Albanian mercenaries in Ottoman service, leaving Egypt politically fragmented.&#x20;

In 1805, [Muhammad Ali Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_of_Egypt) seized power, massacring the remaining Mamluks and establishing a dynasty that would rule Egypt until 1952. He reorganised the army along European lines, introduced conscription, and developed a centralised administration. At the same time, he promoted cash-crop agriculture, especially long-staple cotton, to integrate Egypt into global markets.[^cite_note-Nejla_M._Abu_Izzeddin_1973,_p_2-72] His successors, including [Ibrahim Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Pasha_of_Egypt), [Abbas I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_I_of_Egypt), [Sa'id](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%27id_of_Egypt), and [Isma'il Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma%27il_Pasha_of_Egypt), continued reforms in agriculture, science, and industry, and abolished slavery.&#x20;

Muhammad Ali expanded Egypt's control over [Northern Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Sudan) (1820–1824), [Syria](7515849.md) (1833), and parts of [Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia) and [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia), but European powers intervened in 1841, forcing him to relinquish most of his conquests. He modernised Egypt's infrastructure, built factories and irrigation networks, and strengthened the military, while broader education remained largely limited to military and technical training.[^cite_note-auto-73] The centralisation of power and focus on military and economic modernisation laid the foundation for Egypt's transformation into a regional power.&#x20;

### Monarchical Egypt (1867–1952)

Main articles: [ Khedivate of Egypt (1867–1914)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khedivate_of_Egypt), [ Sultanate of Egypt (1914–1922)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Egypt), and [Kingdom of Egypt (1922–1953)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Egypt_\(1922%E2%80%931953\))

In 1867, Egypt was formally granted autonomous status as a [vassal state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khedivate_of_Egypt) of the Ottoman Empire. The [Suez Canal](29323.md), completed in 1869 with French assistance, became a key strategic and commercial asset. Financial mismanagement and mounting debts led Isma'il Pasha to sell Egypt's shares in the canal to Britain in 1875, increasing European influence. Rising discontent culminated in the [Urabi revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urabi_revolt), after which Britain occupied Egypt in 1882, establishing a de facto protectorate while maintaining nominal Ottoman sovereignty.[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76] The Anglo-Egyptian Condominium Agreement of 1899 placed Sudan under joint Egyptian and British administration, though Britain retained real control. Incidents such as the [Denshawai incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denshawai_incident) in 1906 intensified nationalist sentiment, laying the groundwork for political movements that challenged both Ottoman and European dominance.&#x20;



Female nationalists demonstrating in [Cairo](6293.md) during the [1919 revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Revolution_of_1919), 1919

In 1914, as the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) joined World War I alongside the Central Powers, Khedive [Abbas II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_II_of_Egypt) declared support for the Ottoman side. In response, the British deposed him and installed his brother [Hussein Kamel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_Kamel_of_Egypt), who assumed the title of [Sultan of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Egypt). Egypt was formally declared independent from the Ottoman Empire but remained under British protection.[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78]&#x20;

After [World War I](4764461.md), nationalist sentiments surged. [Saad Zaghloul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saad_Zaghloul) and the [Wafd Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wafd_Party) secured popular support, but the British exiled Zaghloul and his colleagues to Malta on 8 March 1919, prompting the [first modern revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_revolution_of_1919). This uprising pressured the UK to issue a [declaration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unilateral_Declaration_of_Egyptian_Independence) on 22 February 1922.[^cite_note-jankowski-112-79] Sultan [Fuad I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuad_I_of_Egypt) then assumed the title of King of Egypt. Despite nominal independence, Britain retained military presence and political influence.[^cite_note-jankowski-112-79]&#x20;

In 1923, a new constitution established a [parliamentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system) government. The Wafd Party won a decisive victory in the 1923–24 elections, with Saad Zaghloul becoming prime minister. The [1936 treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Egyptian_Treaty_of_1936) led to British troop withdrawal from most of Egypt, except the Suez Canal. The treaty left the status of [Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Egyptian_Sudan) unresolved, as real control remained with Britain.[^cite_note-ModernSudan-80]&#x20;



[Fuad I of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuad_I_of_Egypt) with [Edward, Prince of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_VIII), 1932

During [World War II](32927.md), Egypt served as a strategic base for [Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) operations, particularly in [North Africa](21714.md). Although Egypt declared martial law and severed diplomatic relations with [Axis powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers), the Egyptian army did not engage directly. Political tensions persisted, highlighted by the 1942 [Abdeen Palace Incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdeen_Palace_Incident_of_1942), in which British forces pressured King Farouk to install a Wafd-coalition government.[^cite_note-Britro-81]&#x20;

After the war, nationalist and anti-British sentiments intensified. British troops largely withdrew in 1947, leaving a residual presence around the Suez Canal. Egypt's defeat in the [First Arab-Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Arab-Israeli_War) fuelled anti-monarchy feeling. The Wafd Party's 1950 election victory forced King Farouk to appoint [Mostafa El-Nahas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mostafa_El-Nahas) as prime minister. In 1951, Egypt unilaterally renounced the [1936 treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Egyptian_treaty_of_1936) and demanded British troop withdrawal. The situation around the Suez Canal escalated, culminating in violent confrontations that led to the killing of 43 Egyptian policemen in 1952 by British troops. The Ismailia incident outraged Egypt and led to the subsequent [Black Saturday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo_fire) anti-British riots, which saw widespread destruction in Cairo.[^cite_note-factbook-his-82]&#x20;

These events precipitated the [Free Officers Movement coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Revolution_of_1952) on 22–23 July 1952, led by [Muhammad Naguib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Naguib) and [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser). King Farouk abdicated in favour of his infant son, [Fuad II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuad_II_of_Egypt), but real power rested with the [Egyptian Revolutionary Command Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Revolutionary_Command_Council). By 18 June 1953, the monarchy was formally abolished, the 1923 constitution suspended, and Egypt was declared a republic, with Naguib as president and Nasser as prime minister.[^cite_note-factbook-his-82]&#x20;

### Republican Egypt under Nasser (1952–1970)

Main articles: [History of republican Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_republican_Egypt) and [History of Egypt under Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Egypt_under_Gamal_Abdel_Nasser)

Two smiling men in military uniform seated in an open-top automobile. The first man on the left is pointing his hand in a gesture. Behind the automobile are men in uniform walking away from the vehicle

[Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser) (right) and [Mohamed Naguib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Naguib) (left) during celebrations marking the second anniversary of the [1952 Egyptian revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_revolution_of_1952), July 1954

Following the [1952 Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_revolution_of_1952) led by the [Free Officers Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Officers_Movement_\(Egypt\)), Egypt came under military control, and all political parties were banned. On 18 June 1953, the [Republic of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Egypt_\(1953%E2%80%931958\)) was officially declared, with [Muhammad Naguib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Naguib) serving as the first President. His presidency lasted less than a year and a half, as [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser), a [Pan-Arabist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabist) and the principal architect of the 1952 movement, gradually consolidated power. Naguib was forced to resign in 1954 and placed under [house arrest](247643.md). The presidency remained vacant until Nasser was formally elected in 1956.[^cite_note-83]&#x20;

In October 1954, Egypt and the United Kingdom agreed to end the [Anglo-Egyptian Condominium Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Egyptian_Sudan) of 1899, granting Sudan full independence, which came into effect on 1 January 1956. In June 1956, Nasser assumed the presidency and immediately became the central figure in Egypt's domestic and foreign policy. British forces completed their withdrawal from the Suez Canal Zone on 13 June 1956. Later that year, on 26 July, Nasser [nationalised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalised) the Suez Canal, provoking the [Suez Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis) when Israel, with support from France and the United Kingdom, invaded the Sinai Peninsula and targeted the Canal. The conflict ended following diplomatic pressure from the United States and the Soviet Union, restoring the pre-war status quo.[^cite_note-84]&#x20;



Egyptian President [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser) in [Mansoura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansoura,_Egypt), 1960

In 1958, Egypt formed a political union with Syria, creating the [United Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic). The union, also loosely connected with [North Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutawakkilite_Kingdom_of_Yemen) in the [United Arab States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_States), was short-lived; Syria seceded in 1961. During this period, Egypt became heavily involved in the [North Yemen Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Yemen_Civil_War), with military interventions and peace conferences ultimately leading to a prolonged stalemate.[^cite_note-85] In May 1967, tensions with Israel escalated after warnings from the Soviet Union, deployment of Egyptian forces to Sinai, expulsion of UN peacekeepers, and closure of the [Straits of Tiran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straits_of_Tiran). These measures precipitated the [Six-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Arab-Israeli_War), during which Israel captured the [Sinai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula) and the [Gaza Strip](12047.md). In response to the conflict, an [Emergency Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_law_in_Egypt) was enacted, greatly expanding police powers, restricting constitutional rights, and legalising censorship; it remained in force until 2012, except for a brief break in 1980–81.[^cite_note-86]&#x20;

Socially and economically, Nasser's policies transformed Egypt. At the time of the monarchy's fall, less than half a million Egyptians were considered upper class, four million were middle class, and seventeen million were lower class or poor.[^cite_note-Tarek_Osman_2010,_p._120-87] Education was expanded, with school enrolment more than doubling from 1953 to 1966. Land reforms, industrial support, and growth in public-sector employment created a larger middle class, including doctors, engineers, teachers, lawyers, and journalists. However, by the late 1960s, the Egyptian economy faced stagnation, political freedoms had declined, and Nasser's personal popularity began to wane.[^cite_note-Tarek_Osman_2010,_p._120-87][^cite_note-88]&#x20;

### Egypt under Sadat and Mubarak (1970–2011)

Main articles: [History of Egypt under Anwar Sadat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Egypt_under_Anwar_Sadat) and [History of Egypt under Hosni Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Egypt_under_Hosni_Mubarak)



Egyptian vehicles advancing in the [Sinai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula) during the [Yom Kippur War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War), 1973



Egyptian President [Anwar Sadat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_Sadat) and [Minister of War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defense_\(Egypt\)) [Ahmad Ismail Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Ismail_Ali) attending the re-opening ceremony of Suez Canal after the Yom Kippur war, 1975

After the death of [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser) in 1970, [Anwar Sadat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_Sadat) assumed the presidency of Egypt. Sadat consolidated his power by marginalising Nasserist and leftist factions, while controlling opposition both secular and religious. He shifted Egypt's Cold War alignment from the Soviet Union to the United States, expelling Soviet advisors in 1972, and renamed the country the Arab Republic of Egypt in 1971. Sadat launched the [Infitah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infitah) ("open door") economic reforms.[^cite_note-89] Some measures of this economic liberalisation caused social tensions, most notably the removal of food subsidies in 1977 which sparked widespread [bread riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Egyptian_bread_riots).&#x20;

In 1973, Sadat coordinated with Syria in the [Fourth Arab-Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Arab-Israeli_War) to reclaim the [Sinai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula) from Israeli occupation. Though militarily mixed, the war restored Arab morale and strengthened Sadat's domestic legitimacy. His historic 1977 visit to Jerusalem led to the 1979 [Egypt-Israel peace treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt%E2%80%93Israel_peace_treaty), with Israel withdrawing from Sinai and Egypt recognising Israel as a sovereign state. This initiative provoked widespread controversy across the Arab world, resulting in Egypt's temporary expulsion from the [Arab League](52625.md), but remained popular domestically.[^cite_note-90] Sadat was assassinated in 1981 by an Islamic extremist opposed to his domestic policies and peace initiative.&#x20;

[Hosni Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosni_Mubarak) succeeded Sadat in a 1981 referendum in which he was the sole candidate.[^cite_note-nytimes.com-91] He maintained Egypt's peace treaty with Israel and improved relations with Arab neighbours. Domestically, he faced widespread poverty, high unemployment, and urban overcrowding. The 1986 Security Police riots, sparked by reports of extended military service, led to violent protests, destruction of businesses, and 107 deaths.[^cite_note-92]&#x20;

Terrorist attacks, particularly by Islamist groups like [Al-Gama'a al-Islamiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Gama%27a_al-Islamiyya), targeted government officials, foreigners, and Christian [Copts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copts), devastating tourism, Egypt's primary source of hard currency.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] The political scene was dominated by the [NDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Party_\(Egypt\)), which curtailed freedoms of association, expression, and political participation through laws such as the 1993 Syndicates Law, 1995 Press Law, and 1999 NGOs Law.[^cite_note-95]&#x20;



Cairo grew into a [metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_area) with a population of over 22 million.

[^cite_note-citypopulation2025-96]

In 1997, the [Luxor massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxor_massacre) left 62 dead, mostly tourists. Security and the economy remained under threat. In 2005, limited reforms allowed multi-candidate presidential elections, but restrictions on candidates and alleged government interference ensured Mubarak's easy reelection. Voter turnout was less than 25%, and opposition leader [Ayman Nour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayman_Nour) was subsequently imprisoned.[^cite_note-97]&#x20;

[Human Rights Watch](46530.md) and [Amnesty International](18947898.md) reported widespread human rights abuses, including torture, arbitrary detention, and the use of Egypt as an international "torture hub" in the context of the [war on terror](13425800.md).[^cite_note-HRW-98][^cite_note-99] Constitutional changes in 2007 further expanded presidential powers, restricted religious parties, and broadened police authority. Egypt remained under strong military influence, described by officials as a "pharaonic" system, with democracy as a distant aspiration.[^cite_note-100]&#x20;

### Contemporary (2011–present)

Main articles: [2011 Egyptian revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Egyptian_revolution), [Egyptian Crisis (2011–2014)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Crisis_\(2011%E2%80%932014\)), [2013 Egyptian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Egyptian_protests), and [2013 Egyptian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Egyptian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat)





**Top:** Celebrations in [Tahrir Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahrir_Square) after the announcement of [Hosni Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosni_Mubarak)'s resignation, 2011.\
**Bottom:** Protests in Tahrir Square against [Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi), 2012.

On 25 January 2011, [widespread protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Egyptian_revolution) erupted against President Hosni Mubarak's government, triggered by demands for political freedom, social justice, and the end of long-standing corruption. Demonstrations rapidly spread across major cities, particularly in [Cairo](6293.md)'s [Tahrir Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahrir_Square), capturing global attention.[^cite_note-101] After 18 days of escalating unrest, Mubarak resigned on 11 February 2011 and fled Cairo. The [Egyptian military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_military) assumed control, with [Mohamed Hussein Tantawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Hussein_Tantawi), chairman of the [Supreme Council of the Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Council_of_the_Armed_Forces), acting as interim head of state.[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103] On 13 February, the military dissolved parliament and suspended the constitution.[^cite_note-104] A constitutional referendum followed on 19 March 2011.[^cite_note-105] Later that year, Egypt held its first parliamentary elections since the previous regime, with high voter turnout and no major reported irregularities.[^cite_note-106]&#x20;

[Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi), affiliated with the [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md), won the [presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Egyptian_presidential_election) on 24 June 2012 and was sworn in on 30 June.[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108] His cabinet, announced in August 2012, included significant representation from the Muslim Brotherhood, prompting liberal and secular groups to withdraw from the [Constituent Assembly of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_Egypt) over concerns of imposing strict Islamic law.[^cite_note-109] In November 2012, Morsi issued a decree granting immunity to his decisions and protecting the constituent assembly's work, sparking [mass protests and violent clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012%E2%80%932013_Egyptian_protests) across the country.[^cite_note-110] Tensions escalated, with the largest confrontations between Islamist supporters and opponents since the 2011 revolution occurring on 5 December 2012.[^cite_note-111] Morsi refused to cancel the December 2012 [constitutional referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Egyptian_constitutional_referendum).[^cite_note-112]&#x20;

Following growing public discontent, the [military removed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Egyptian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) on 3 July 2013, dissolved the [Shura Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_\(Egypt\)), and installed [Adly Mansour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adly_Mansour), chief justice of the [Supreme Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Constitutional_Court_\(Egypt\)), as interim president.[^cite_note-113] Authorities cracked down on the Muslim Brotherhood, jailing thousands and conducting mass trials. Violence during [dispersals of pro-Morsi camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabaa_massacre) left hundreds dead.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115] A new [constitution](41678239.md) was approved by referendum on 18 January 2014 with 98.1% voting in favour.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117]&#x20;

[Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md) won the [presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Egyptian_presidential_election) in June 2014 with 96.1% of the vote and was sworn in on 8 June. Under his rule, Egypt intensified security on the Gaza border, dismantled tunnels between Sinai and Gaza, and consolidated political power. Presidential terms were extended to six years in 2019, allowing El-Sisi to run for a third term in 2024. Parliamentary elections in 2020 confirmed a pro-El-Sisi majority for the [Mostaqbal Watan Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation%27s_Future_Party). The constitutional reforms and strengthened military authority under El-Sisi have been described as a return to authoritarianism.[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119] In December 2023, El-Sisi won the elections that gave him a third six-year term.[^cite_note-120]&#x20;

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Egypt)



Egypt's topography

Egypt lies primarily between latitudes [22°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/22nd_parallel_north) and [32°N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/32nd_parallel_north), and longitudes [25°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25th_meridian_east) and [35°E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/35th_meridian_east). At 1,001,450 square kilometres (386,660 sq mi), it is the world's 29th largest country.[^cite_note-121] Due to the extreme aridity of Egypt's climate, population centres are concentrated along the narrow Nile Valley and Delta, meaning that about 99% of the population uses about 5.5% of the total land area.[^cite_note-122] 98% of Egyptians live on 3% of the territory.[^cite_note-123]&#x20;



The [Qattara Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qattara_Depression) in Egypt's north west

Egypt is bordered by Libya to the west, the Sudan to the south, and the Gaza Strip and Israel to the east. A [transcontinental nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcontinental_nation), it possesses a land bridge (the Isthmus of Suez) between Africa and Asia, traversed by a navigable waterway (the [Suez Canal](29323.md)) that [connects the Mediterranean Sea with the Indian Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Mediterranean) by way of the Red Sea.&#x20;

Apart from the Nile Valley, the majority of Egypt's landscape is desert, with a few [oases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oasis) scattered about. Winds create prolific [sand dunes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dune) that peak at more than 30 metres (100 ft) high. Egypt includes parts of the [Sahara](325363.md) desert and of the [Libyan Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Desert).&#x20;

Sinai peninsula hosts the highest mountain in Egypt, [Mount Catherine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Catherine) at 2,642 metres. The [Red Sea Riviera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Sea_Riviera), on the east of the peninsula, is renowned for its wealth of coral reefs and marine life.&#x20;

Towns and cities include [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), the second largest city; [Aswan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aswan); [Asyut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asyut); [Cairo](6293.md), the modern Egyptian capital and largest city; [El Mahalla El Kubra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Mahalla_El_Kubra); [Giza](144514.md), the site of the Pyramid of Khufu; [Hurghada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurghada); [Luxor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxor); [Kom Ombo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kom_Ombo); [Port Safaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Safaga); [Port Said](205162.md); [Sharm El Sheikh](419335.md); [Suez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez), where the south end of the Suez Canal is located; [Zagazig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagazig); and [Minya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minya,_Egypt). [Oases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oasis) include [Bahariya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahariya_Oasis), [Dakhla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakhla_Oasis), [Farafra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farafra,_Egypt), [Kharga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharga_Oasis) and [Siwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siwa_Oasis). [Protectorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectorates) include Ras Mohamed National Park, Zaranik Protectorate and Siwa.&#x20;

On 13 March 2015, plans for a [proposed new capital of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_new_capital_of_Egypt) were announced.[^cite_note-124]&#x20;

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Egypt)



The sharp boundary between the hyper-arid [desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Desert) and the irrigated [Nile Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile_Valley), where most of Egypt's population and agriculture are concentrated.

Most of Egypt's rain falls in the winter months.[^cite_note-125] South of Cairo, rainfall averages only around 2 to 5 mm (0.1 to 0.2 in) per year and at intervals of many years. On a very thin strip of the northern coast the rainfall can be as high as 410 mm (16.1 in),[^cite_note-126] mostly between October and March. Snow falls on Sinai's mountains and some of the north coastal cities such as [Damietta](379680.md), [Baltim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltim) and [Sidi Barrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidi_Barrani), and rarely in Alexandria. A very small amount of snow fell on Cairo on 13 December 2013, the first time in many decades.[^cite_note-127] [Frost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frost) is also known in mid-Sinai and mid-Egypt.&#x20;

Egypt has a very hot, sunny and dry climate. Average temperature highs are very high to extremely high throughout most of the country. The exception to this is the northern part of the country, which, although still hot, has its temperatures moderated by the cool Mediterranean winds consistently blowing in over the sea coast, especially at the height of summer. The [Khamaseen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khamaseen) is a hot, dry wind that originates from the vast deserts in the south and blows in the spring or in the early summer. It brings scorching sand and dust particles, and usually brings daytime temperatures over 40 °C (104 °F) and sometimes over 50 °C (122 °F) in the interior, while the relative humidity can drop to 5% or even less.&#x20;

Prior to the construction of the [Aswan Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aswan_Dam), the Nile flooded annually, replenishing Egypt's soil. This gave Egypt a consistent harvest throughout the years.&#x20;

Egypt's hot and arid climate is under increasing strain from climate change, which has brought extreme temperatures, droughts, floods, and rising sea levels.[^cite_note-128] Egypt is highly vulnerable, and these shifts threaten food security, water availability, public health, and economic stability.[^cite_note-129][^cite_note-130]&#x20;

### Biodiversity

Main article: [Wildlife of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Egypt)



The [Eastern Imperial Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Imperial_Eagle) is the national animal of Egypt.

Egypt signed the Rio [Convention on Biological Diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_Biological_Diversity) on 9 June 1992, and became a party to the convention on 2 June 1994.[^cite_note-131] It produced a [National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_Action_Plan) which was received by the convention on 31 July 1998.[^cite_note-132]&#x20;

The plan stated that the following numbers of species of different groups had been recorded from Egypt: algae (1483 species), animals (about 15,000 species of which more than 10,000 were insects), fungi (more than 627 species), monera (319 species), plants (2426 species), protozoans (371 species). For some major groups, for example lichen-forming fungi and nematode worms, the number was not known. Apart from small and well-studied groups like amphibians, birds, fish, mammals and reptiles, the many of those numbers are likely to increase as further species are recorded from Egypt. For the fungi, including lichen-forming species, for example, subsequent work has shown that over 2200 species have been recorded from Egypt, and the final figure of all fungi actually occurring in the country is expected to be much higher.[^cite_note-133] For the grasses, 284 native and naturalised species have been identified and recorded in Egypt.[^cite_note-134]&#x20;

## Government

Main article: [Politics of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Egypt)



[Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md)\
[President](335341.md)&#x20;



[Mostafa Madbouly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mostafa_Madbouly)\
[Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Egypt)&#x20;

The [House of Representatives](1912306.md), whose members are elected to serve five-year terms, specialises in legislation. [Elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Egypt) were held between [November 2011 and January 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011%E2%80%9312_Egyptian_parliamentary_election), which were later dissolved.&#x20;

After a wave of public discontent with the [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md) government of President [Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi),[^cite_note-morsi-think-again-135] on 3 July 2013 then-[General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Army_ranks) [Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md) announced the removal of Morsi from office and the suspension of the [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Constitution_of_2012). A 50-member constitution committee was formed for modifying [the constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Constitution_of_2014) which was later published for [public voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_constitutional_referendum,_2014) and was adopted on 18 January 2014.[^cite_note-136]&#x20;

[The next parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_parliamentary_election,_2015) was announced to be held within 6 months of the constitution's ratification on 18 January 2014, and were held in two phases, from 17 October to 2 December 2015.[^cite_note-ao30aug-137] Originally, the parliament was to be formed before the president was elected, but interim president [Adly Mansour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adly_Mansour) pushed the date.[^cite_note-138] The [2014 Egyptian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Egyptian_presidential_election) took place on 26–28 May. Official figures showed a turnout of 25,578,233 or 47.5%, with Abdel Fattah el-Sisi winning with 23.78 million votes, or 96.9% compared to 757,511 (3.1%) for [Hamdeen Sabahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamdeen_Sabahi).[^cite_note-139]&#x20;

In 2024, as part of its [Freedom in the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_in_the_World) report, [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) rated [political rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_rights) in Egypt at 6 (with 40 representing the most free and 0 the least), and [civil liberties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_liberties) at 12 (with 60 being the highest score and 0 the lowest), which gave it the freedom rating of "Not Free".[^cite_note-140] According to the 2023 [V-Dem Democracy indices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_indices) Egypt is the eighth least [democratic country in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_Africa).[^cite_note-vdem_dataset-141] The 2023 edition of [The Economist Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist_Democracy_Index) categorises Egypt as an "authoritarian regime", with a score of 2.93.[^cite_note-142]&#x20;

Egypt has the oldest continuous parliamentary tradition in the Arab world.[^cite_note-pogar-143] The first popular assembly was established in 1866. It was disbanded as a result of the British occupation of 1882, and the British allowed only a consultative body to sit. In 1923, however, after the country's independence was declared, a new constitution provided for a parliamentary monarchy.[^cite_note-pogar-143]&#x20;

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Egypt)





Top: Former President [Hosni Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosni_Mubarak) with former US President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) at Camp David in 2002.\
Bottom: President [Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md) and Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) at the [16th BRICS Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_BRICS_Summit) in 2024

Egypt's foreign policy is shaped by its strategic geographical position, historical legacy, and diplomatic influence in Africa, the Mediterranean, and Southwest Asia. As a founding member of the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement) and the United Nations, Egypt has maintained a non-aligned stance while remaining active in regional diplomacy. Cairo, historically a crossroads of commerce and culture, remains a centre of intellectual and political influence in the region.&#x20;

The [United States](15982815.md) has been a key partner, providing Egypt with US\$1.3 billion in annual military assistance as of 2015.[^cite_note-144] In 1989, Egypt was designated a [major non-NATO ally](3812445.md), but relations soured following the [2013 Egyptian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Egyptian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat), as the [Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obama_administration) criticised Egypt's crackdown on the [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md) and suspended joint military exercises.[^cite_note-145] Under President [Donald Trump](4848272.md), relations improved, with [Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md)'s 2017 visit to the White House marking the first by an Egyptian president in eight years.[^cite_note-146]&#x20;

Relations with [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt%E2%80%93Russia_relations) strengthened after Morsi's removal, with both nations expanding military cooperation[^cite_note-147] and trade relations.[^cite_note-148] Similarly, [relations with China](27704421.md) have deepened, culminating in the establishment of a "comprehensive strategic partnership" in 2014.[^cite_note-149]&#x20;

In 2024, Egypt and the [European Union](9317.md) elevated their relationship to a "strategic and comprehensive partnership", emphasising political cooperation, and economic collaboration. Both sides are also working on deepening trade relations under the Association Agreement, supporting Egypt's economic stability, and strengthening counterterrorism efforts.[^cite_note-150]&#x20;

Egypt remains an influential mediator in Middle Eastern conflicts, particularly in brokering ceasefires in Gaza and facilitating the [Israeli–Palestinian peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_peace_process).[^cite_note-151] It is also a Member State of the [African Union](10634933.md) and the [Arab League](52625.md), hosting the Arab League headquarters in Cairo. The Arab League temporarily moved to Tunis in 1978 in protest of the [Egypt–Israel peace treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt%E2%80%93Israel_peace_treaty), but returned to Cairo in 1989. Egypt has also cultivated strong economic ties with Gulf nations such as [Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_relations) and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md), both of which have provided billions of dollars in financial assistance.[^cite_note-152]&#x20;

### Military

Main article: [Egyptian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Armed_Forces)

Further information: [Military industry of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_industry_of_Egypt)



[Egyptian honour guard soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Republican_Guard) in 2009

The [Egyptian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Armed_Forces) constitute Egypt's military establishment, comprising the [Egyptian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Army), [Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Navy), [Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Air_Force), and [Air Defence Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Air_Defence_Forces) under the direct supervision of the Chief of Staff,[^cite_note-153] with their headquarters at the [State Strategic Command Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Strategic_Command_Center), commonly known as the Octagon, in the [New Administrative Capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Administrative_Capital). The military primarily oversees external defence but also plays a domestic role, assisting police in protecting vital infrastructure during emergencies and counterterrorism operations.[^cite_note-NYT_2011-154] They also participate in foreign peacekeeping missions and engage in bilateral and multinational military exercises.[^cite_note-CIA-military-155]&#x20;

The military wields significant political influence, operating autonomously and remaining exempt from many civilian laws.[^cite_note-NYT_2011-154] A significant amount of military information is not made publicly available, including budget information, the names of the general officers and the military's size (which is considered a state secret).[^cite_note-NYT_2011-154] In addition, the military is a major driver of Egypt's economic landscape, engaging in infrastructure development, consumer goods production, and real estate holdings through the [National Service Projects Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Service_Products_Organization).&#x20;

With an active personnel strength of 438,500 and 479,000 reservists,[^cite_note-156] it is one of the largest and best-equipped militaries in the region.[^cite_note-CIA-military-155] Conscription is mandatory for men aged 18–30, with service obligations ranging from 14 to 36 months, depending on educational level, followed by a nine-year reserve duty. Voluntary enlistment is possible from age 16 for men and 17 for women.[^cite_note-CIA-military-155]&#x20;

The military operates a diverse arsenal sourced primarily from China, France, Germany, Italy, Russia, and the United States. Recent efforts have focused on modernisation and procurement of advanced weaponry.[^cite_note-CIA-military-155] The domestic defence industry is also well-established, manufacturing small arms, armoured vehicles, and naval vessels, while maintaining licensed production agreements with countries such as [Germany](11867.md) and the [United States](3434750.md).[^cite_note-CIA-military-155]&#x20;

Egypt is one of few countries in the region to possess spy satellites, launching [EgyptSat 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EgyptSat_1) in 2007,[^cite_note-157] followed by [EgyptSat 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EgyptSat_2) in 2014.[^cite_note-158] In 2019, Egypt launched MisrSat-A, an observation satellite,[^cite_note-159] followed later that year by Tiba-1, a civilian and military communications satellite.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] In 2023, Egypt launched Horus-1 and Horus-2, about a month apart. The country also placed MisrSat-2 into orbit later that year. All three of them are high-resolution observation satellites.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164] In 2024, two months after MisrSat-2's launch, the country successfully completed the initial operational phase of NEXSAT-1, its first experimental Earth observation satellite, achieving milestones such as developing indigenous onboard software, advanced attitude-control systems, and independently capturing and processing satellite imagery, all of which marked a major step forward in Egypt's national space capabilities.[^cite_note-165]&#x20;

### Law

Main article: [Egyptian Civil Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Civil_Code)



The High Court of Justice in [Downtown Cairo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Cairo)

The legal system is based on [Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharia_law) and civil law (particularly the [Napoleonic Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Code)); and judicial review by a Supreme Court, which accepts compulsory [International Court of Justice](14918.md) jurisdiction only with reservations.[^cite_note-factbook-his-82] The highest judicial authority of Egypt is the [Supreme Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Constitutional_Court_\(Egypt\)), located in the [Maadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maadi) district of Cairo.&#x20;

Islamic jurisprudence is the principal source of legislation. Sharia courts and qadis are run and licensed by the [Ministry of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(Egypt\)).[^cite_note-166] The personal status law that regulates matters such as marriage, divorce and child custody is governed by Sharia. In a family court, a woman's testimony is worth half of a man's testimony.[^cite_note-167]&#x20;

On 26 December 2012, the Muslim Brotherhood attempted to institutionalise a controversial new constitution. It was approved by the public in a [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_constitutional_referendum,_2012) held 15–22 December 2012 with 64% support, but with only 33% electorate participation.[^cite_note-168] It replaced the [2011 Provisional Constitution of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Provisional_Constitution_of_Egypt), adopted following the revolution.&#x20;

The Penal code was unique as it contains a "[Blasphemy Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blasphemy_law_in_Egypt)".[^cite_note-169] The present court system allows a death penalty including against an absent individual [tried *in absentia*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tried_in_absentia). Several Americans and Canadians were sentenced to death in 2012.[^cite_note-170]&#x20;

On 18 January 2014, the interim government successfully institutionalised a more [secular constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Constitution_of_2014).[^cite_note-BBC-171] The president is elected to a four-year term and may serve 2 terms.[^cite_note-BBC-171] The parliament may impeach the president.[^cite_note-BBC-171] Under the constitution, there is a guarantee of gender equality and absolute [freedom of thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_Religion).[^cite_note-BBC-171] The military retains the ability to appoint the national Minister of Defence for the next two full presidential terms since the constitution took effect.[^cite_note-BBC-171] Under the constitution, political parties may not be based on "religion, race, gender or geography".[^cite_note-BBC-171]&#x20;

#### Human rights

Main article: [Human rights in Egypt](6184863.md)

See also: [August 2013 Rabaa massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_2013_Rabaa_massacre) and [Persecution of Copts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Copts)



Protesters from the Third Square movement, which supported neither the former Morsi government nor the Armed Forces, 31 July 2013

The [National Council for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_for_Human_Rights) was established in 2003, but it has faced criticism from activists who argue it serves as a government propaganda tool to justify human rights violations and repressive laws such as the Emergency Law.[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174]&#x20;

Egypt has been ranked among the worst countries for religious freedom, with widespread discrimination against minorities such as [Coptic Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_Christians), [Baháʼís](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%ADs), and other Muslim sects. A 2009 report by [Pew Forum on Religion & Public Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Forum_on_Religion_%26_Public_Life) ranked Egypt fifth worst globally for religious freedom,[^cite_note-175] while the [United States Commission on International Religious Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Commission_on_International_Religious_Freedom) has placed Egypt on its watch list due to state-tolerated violations.[^cite_note-176] A 2010 [Pew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) survey found that 84% of Egyptians supported the death penalty for [apostasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam), 77% backed amputations for theft, and 82% endorsed stoning for [adultery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adultery).[^cite_note-177]&#x20;

Since the [2013 Egyptian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Egyptian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat), political repression has intensified, with mass arrests and harsh crackdowns on dissent. During the [August 2013 sit-in dispersal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_2013_Egyptian_raids), 595 protesters were killed, making 14 August 2013 the deadliest day in Egypt's modern history.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] Since then, an estimated 60,000 [political prisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_prisoners_in_Egypt) remain behind bars.[^cite_note-180]&#x20;

Egypt has also drawn international condemnation for mass death sentences, including a 2014 ruling sentencing 529 people to death in a single hearing.[^cite_note-181] The [United Nations](31769.md) and human rights groups have condemned these trials as gross violations of [international law](8195726.md).[^cite_note-182]&#x20;

[Homosexuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Egypt) remains [de facto](43594.md) criminalised, with 95% of Egyptians opposing its acceptance, according to a 2013 [Pew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) survey.[^cite_note-pewglobal.org-183] A poll by the [Thomson Reuters Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomson_Reuters_Foundation), based on assessments from specialists in women's rights, ranked Cairo as the worst megacity for women, evaluating cities on sexual harassment, healthcare access, harmful cultural practices, and economic opportunities.[^cite_note-184]&#x20;

#### Freedom of the press

[Reporters Without Borders](526054.md) ranked Egypt in their 2017 [World Press Freedom Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index) at No. 160 out of 180 nations. At least 18 journalists were imprisoned in Egypt, as of August 2015. A new anti-terror law was enacted in August 2015 that threatens members of the media with fines ranging from about US\$25,000 to \$60,000 for the distribution of wrong information on acts of terror inside the country "that differ from official declarations of the Egyptian Department of Defence".[^cite_note-185]&#x20;

### Administrative divisions

Main article: [Subdivisions of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subdivisions_of_Egypt)

Egypt is divided into 27 governorates. The governorates are further divided into regions. The regions contain towns and villages. Each governorate has a capital, sometimes carrying the same name as the governorate.[^cite_note-Beckouche2017-186]&#x20;
