## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Egypt)

Egypt is a recognised cultural trendsetter of the Arabic-speaking world. Contemporary Arabic and Middle-Eastern culture is heavily influenced by Egyptian literature, music, film and television. Egypt gained a regional leadership role during the 1950s and 1960s, giving a further enduring boost to the standing of Egyptian culture in the Arabic-speaking world.[^cite_note-326]&#x20;



[Al-Azhar Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Azhar_Park) is listed as one of the world's sixty great public spaces by the [Project for Public Spaces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_for_Public_Spaces).

Egyptian identity evolved in the span of a long period of occupation to accommodate [Islam](6037917.md), Christianity and Judaism; and a new language, [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language), and its spoken descendant, [Egyptian Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Arabic), which has a significant [Coptic-Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_language) [substrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratum_\(linguistics\)).[^cite_note-327]&#x20;

The work of early 19th century scholar [Rifa'a al-Tahtawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rifa%27a_al-Tahtawi) renewed interest in [Egyptian antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt) and exposed Egyptian society to [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) principles. Tahtawi co-founded with education reformer [Ali Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Mubarak) a native [Egyptology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptology) school that looked for inspiration to medieval Egyptian scholars, such as [Suyuti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suyuti) and [Maqrizi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqrizi), who themselves studied the [history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_ancient_Egypt), [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_language) and [antiquities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egyptian_architecture) of Egypt.[^cite_note-328]&#x20;

Egypt's renaissance peaked in the late 19th and early 20th centuries through the work of people like [Muhammad Abduh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Abduh), [Ahmed Lutfi el-Sayed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Lutfi_el-Sayed), [Muhammad Loutfi Goumah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Loutfi_Goumah), [Tawfiq el-Hakim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawfiq_el-Hakim), [Louis Awad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Awad), [Qasim Amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qasim_Amin), [Salama Moussa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salama_Moussa), [Taha Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taha_Hussein) and [Mahmoud Mokhtar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_Mokhtar). They forged a liberal path for Egypt expressed as a commitment to personal freedom, [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) and faith in science to bring progress.[^cite_note-329]&#x20;

### Arts

Main articles: [Contemporary art in Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_art_in_Egypt) and [Art of ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_ancient_Egypt)

See also: [Architecture of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Egypt)



The "weighing of the heart" scene from the *[Book of the Dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_the_Dead)*

The Egyptians were one of the first major civilisations to codify design elements in art and [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egyptian_architecture). [Egyptian blue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_blue), also known as calcium copper silicate, is a pigment used by Egyptians for thousands of years. It is considered to be the first synthetic pigment. The wall paintings done in the service of the [Pharaohs](23294.md) followed a rigid code of visual rules and meanings. Egyptian civilisation is renowned for its colossal [pyramids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_pyramids), [temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_temple) and monumental tombs. Well-known examples are the [Pyramid of Djoser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyramid_of_Djoser) designed by ancient architect and engineer [Imhotep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imhotep), the [Sphinx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphinx), and the temple of [Abu Simbel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Simbel).&#x20;

Modern and contemporary Egyptian art spans disciplines from the vernacular architecture of [Hassan Fathy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Fathy) and [Ramses Wissa Wassef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramses_Wissa_Wassef) to the iconic sculptures of [Mahmoud Mokhtar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_Mokhtar) and the distinctive [Coptic iconography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_art) of [Isaac Fanous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Fanous). Its development in the twentieth century reflected a complex dialogue between national identity, global artistic trends, and social change. Early modern Egyptian artists sought to reconnect with their national heritage through Neo-Pharaonic styles in architecture, sculpture, and painting, with artists such as Mahmoud Mokhtar and [Mahmoud Sa'id](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_Sa%27id) incorporating symbolic references to ancient Egypt and rural life.[^cite_note-met-mikdadi-330][^cite_note-Engelstad-331]&#x20;

Later generations embraced international movements like [Surrealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrealism), [Cubism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubism), [Dadaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dadaism), and [abstraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_art), while the [Art et Liberté](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_et_Libert%C3%A9) group, including [Ramses Younan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramses_Younan), promoted individual expression and antifascist ideals.[^cite_note-met-mikdadi-330] The Contemporary Art Group, with artists such as [Gazbia Sirry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazbia_Sirry) and [Abdel Hadi Al Gazzar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel_Hadi_Al_Gazzar), explored social realism and the quest for the Egyptian soul, while experimental painters and sculptors like Munir Canaan and Salah Abdel Kerim explored [Abstract Expressionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_Expressionism) and [assemblage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assemblage_\(art\)).[^cite_note-met-mikdadi-330] In the latter half of the century, artists responded to political and cultural shifts with Islamic-inspired calligraphy and geometric abstraction, exemplified by the Calligraphic School of Art.[^cite_note-met-mikdadi-330] Other notable artists from this era include [Inji Efflatoun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inji_Efflatoun) and [Kamal Amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamal_Amin).&#x20;

The launch of institutions such as the [Townhouse Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Townhouse_Gallery) in 1998 democratised art access and introduced new media forms like video and installation art,[^cite_note-332] providing a platform for younger artists such as [Fathi Hassan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fathi_Hassan), [Ghada Amer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghada_Amer), Medhat Shafik, [Moataz Nasr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moataz_Nasr), and Mona Marzouk to experiment with installation, video, and photography.[^cite_note-met-mikdadi-330] The [2011 Egyptian revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Egyptian_revolution) ushered in a new era of politically and socially engaged art. [Graffiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graffiti) emerged as a prominent medium to document protests and communicate revolutionary messages,[^cite_note-333] with artists like [Bahia Shehab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahia_Shehab), [Alaa Awad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaa_Awad), [Aya Tarek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aya_Tarek), and [Ganzeer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganzeer) creating works that memorialised martyrs, critiqued political figures, and reclaimed public spaces.[^cite_note-334]&#x20;





*Khayamiya* design motives.

[Khayamiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khayamiya) is a traditional Egyptian appliqué textile art primarily made in Cairo's historic *ShareʿEl Khayamiya* ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): شارع الخيامية, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *Shāriʿ al-Khayāmiyya*, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) 'Street of the Tentmakers'), also known as the Tentmakers Market. The craft, thought to date back to [ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt), involves a labour-intensive hand-stitching process that can take weeks to complete, with designs ranging from geometric motifs to scenes drawn from Egyptian history and folklore. It was originally associated with decorated tents, but today it is also used for quilts, cushion covers, and wall hangings.[^cite_note-335]&#x20;

The [Cairo Opera House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo_Opera_House) is Egypt's main performing arts venue, with the [Sayed Darwish Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria_Opera_House) in [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria) and the [Port Said Opera House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Said_Opera_House) in [Port Said](205162.md) serving their respective cities. In 2012 the [Downtown Contemporary Arts Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Contemporary_Arts_Festival) (D-CAF) was launched to revitalise Cairo's cultural scene and to provide an inclusive platform for contemporary arts. It is an annual arts festival held in Cairo, Egypt, presenting a range of performances, exhibitions, and workshops in theatre, dance, and visual arts.[^cite_note-336]&#x20;

### Literature

Main article: [Egyptian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_literature)

The literary tradition of Egypt began in [ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt), making it among the earliest in human history. Writing was first used by Egyptians to record texts on materials such as papyrus and carved inscriptions.[^cite_note-337] The *[Story of Sinuhe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Story_of_Sinuhe)* is perhaps its best-known work;[^cite_note-338] and the autobiography has been called the earliest form of Egyptian literature.[^cite_note-339] By the eighth century Egypt became part of the Muslim Arab world. Literature and libraries thrived under the new order, papyrus was replaced by paper, and calligraphy became central.[^cite_note-340] In the 13th century, [Ibn al-Nafis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Nafis) wrote *[Theologus Autodidactus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theologus_Autodidactus)*, a theological novel with proto-science fiction elements.[^cite_note-Roubi-341] Literary practices such as the *taqriz* (commendatory blurbs) appeared in 14th-century Egypt,[^cite_note-342] and Egyptian folklore contributed to *[One Thousand and One Nights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Thousand_and_One_Nights)*.[^cite_note-Burton-1991-343]&#x20;

In the late 19th and early 20th centuries, Egypt was central to the *[Nahda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahda)*, the Arab cultural renaissance.[^cite_note-344] [Muhammad Abduh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Abduh), a leading figure of Islamic modernism,[^cite_note-Meisami_1998-345] co-founded the revolutionary journal *[Al-Urwah al-Wuthqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Urwah_al-Wuthqa)* with [Jamal al-Din al-Afghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamal_al-Din_al-Afghani) in 1884; though quickly banned by the British, it circulated widely across the Arab world.[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-\:02-347][^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349] [Ahmad Shawqi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shawqi) pioneered the expansion of the classical [qasida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qasida) form, though his work remained firmly rooted in neo-classical style. Following him, poets such as [Hafez Ibrahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_Ibrahim) began addressing themes of [anticolonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-imperialism) alongside traditional poetic subjects.[^cite_note-ansari-literature-350] [Muhammad Husayn Haykal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Husayn_Haykal)'s *[Zaynab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaynab_\(novel\))* is considered the first modern Egyptian novel.[^cite_note-351] This novel started a movement of modernising Arabic fiction.[^cite_note-ansari-literature-350] Poetry remained vibrant, with figures such as [Aziz Pasha Abaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aziz_Pasha_Abaza) contributing classical-style verse with [Pan-Arabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabism) themes. The Abaza family produced several notable literary figures, including [Fekry Pasha Abaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fekry_Pasha_Abaza), [Tharwat Abaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tharwat_Abaza), and Desouky Pasha Abaza.[^cite_note-352][^cite_note-353]&#x20;



[Naguib Mahfouz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naguib_Mahfouz), the first Arabic-language writer to win the Nobel Prize in Literature

In the 20th century, modernist movements shaped Egyptian literature. *[Al-Madrasa al-Ḥadītha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Madrasa_al-%E1%B8%A4ad%C4%ABtha)* focused on the short story,[^cite_note-354] while two of the most prominent figures were [Taha Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taha_Hussein) and [Naguib Mahfouz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naguib_Mahfouz), the latter the first Egyptian and Arabic-language writer to receive the [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md). Avant-garde literary activity emerged through magazines like *[Galerie 68](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galerie_68)*, founded by [Edwar al-Kharrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwar_al-Kharrat), giving voice to Egypt's 1960s generation.[^cite_note-355]&#x20;

In the 1990s, Egyptian literature responded to socio-economic changes such as urbanisation and rising living costs, focusing on isolated individuals in a changing society, exemplified by Mustafa Zikri, [Nura Amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nura_Amin), and [May Telmissany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_Telmissany).[^cite_note-Safez-356] This era also saw the rise of women writers, often called *kitabat al-banat* ("girls' writing"), with shorter, first-person narratives.[^cite_note-357][^cite_note-358]&#x20;

Egyptians constitute the largest share of shortlisted authors for the [International Prize for Arabic Fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Prize_for_Arabic_Fiction), with [Bahaa Taher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahaa_Taher)'s *Sunset Oasis* winning the inaugural edition in 2008, followed the next year by [Youssef Ziedan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youssef_Ziedan)'s *Azazeel*.[^cite_note-359] Other notable contemporary authors include [Radwa Ashour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radwa_Ashour), acclaimed for the *[Granada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granada_\(Trilogy\))* trilogy; [Ahdaf Soueif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahdaf_Soueif), whose English-language novel *The Map of Love* was shortlisted for the [Booker Prize](4446.md) in 1999; [Ahmed Khaled Tawfik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Khaled_Tawfik), who introduced horror and science fiction to Egyptian literature with his *Ma Waraa al-Tabiaa* series; and [Ahmed Mourad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Mourad), known for bestselling novels such as *Vertigo* and *[The Blue Elephant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Blue_Elephant_\(novel\))*.[^cite_note-360] Feminist themes are explored by [Nawal El Saadawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawal_El_Saadawi) and [Alifa Rifaat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alifa_Rifaat), while vernacular poetry is represented by [Ahmed Fouad Negm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Fouad_Negm), [Salah Jaheen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah_Jaheen), and [Abdel Rahman el-Abnudi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel_Rahman_el-Abnudi).[^cite_note-361]&#x20;

### Media

Main article: [Media of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Egypt)

See also: [List of radio stations in Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_radio_stations_in_Egypt)



The first issue of [Al-Waqa'i' al-Misriyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Waqa%27i%27_al-Misriyya), printed in 1828 by the [Amiriya Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulaq_Press). It and its predecessor [Jurnal al-Khidiw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurnal_al-Khidiw) are the oldest Arabic-language newspapers.

Egypt is a major regional media hub, with its press among the most influential in the Arab world. The [printing press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Printing_press) was first introduced to Egypt by [Napoleon Bonaparte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon) during his [French Campaign in Egypt and Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Campaign_in_Egypt_and_Syria).[^cite_note-Cole_2007_148-362] [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria) was the centre of Egyptian journalism for much of the 1800s,[^cite_note-kendallp339-363] with many literary journals starting there before moving to [Cairo](6293.md).[^cite_note-364] By the 1890s, Cairo had become dominant, hosting 65% of publications, while Alexandria accounted for 28%.[^cite_note-kendallp339-363] The written press in Egypt today is highly diverse, with more than 600 newspapers, journals, and magazines.[^cite_note-365] The three leading state-owned press institutions are *[Al-Ahram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ahram)*, [*Al-Akhbar*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Akhbar_\(Egypt\)) and *Dar Al-Tahrir,[^cite_note-arabmediasoc-366]* the latter being a major publishing house that issues a wide range of newspapers and magazines in different languages, including *[Al-Gomhuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gomhuria)*, *[The Egyptian Gazette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Egyptian_Gazette)*, *[Le Progrès Egyptien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Progr%C3%A8s_Egyptien),* among others.[^cite_note-367]&#x20;

Egypt was the first country in the region to introduce radio broadcasts to a wide population in 1926, initially through private short-wave stations with limited range, primarily located between [Cairo](6293.md) and [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria).[^cite_note-ahram-radio-368] The official Egyptian radio service launched on 31 May 1934, with the first broadcast featuring [Muhammad Rifat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Rifat), a Quran reciter.[^cite_note-ahram-radio-368] Following the [1952 Egyptian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_Egyptian_Revolution), radio became a central tool of state communication and regional influence. Under President [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser), Egypt developed the [Voice of the Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_the_Arabs) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): صوت العرب, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *Sawt El Arab*) service, launched in the 1950s as one of the most prominent Arabic-language broadcasts of its time.[^cite_note-Boyd1975-369] Initially modest in size, Egypt's radio system was rapidly expanded by the revolutionary government. It was used to promote Arab unity, strengthen Egypt's leadership role in the region, often calling for solidarity and revolutionary action, particularly during the 1950s and 1960s.[^cite_note-Boyd1975-369] Contemporary radio in Egypt is largely operated by the [Egyptian Radio and Television Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Radio_and_Television_Union) (ERTU), established in 1970, which manages multiple national and local stations. Private FM stations also exist, but state radio remains the most widespread. Programming includes news, cultural content, educational programmes.[^cite_note-arabmediasoc-366]&#x20;



The [Maspero building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maspero_television_building) in [Cairo](6293.md), headquarters of the [Egyptian Radio and Television Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Radio_and_Television_Union)

Egyptian television began in 1960 when Channel 1 launched with [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) readings and a speech by President [Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser), followed by Channel 2 in 1961 offering cultural, informational, and instructional programmes. Over the following decades, additional channels were launched regionally.[^cite_note-arabmediasoc-366] In the 21st century, Egypt's television and film industry continue to supply much of the region through Cairo's [Media Production City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Media_Production_City). Television remains the most popular medium in the country, with [ERTU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Radio_and_Television_Union) operating two national terrestrial channels, six local terrestrial channels covering all 27 governorates, three satellite channels, and over ten specialised channels, including news, sport, culture, education, and entertainment.[^cite_note-arabmediasoc-366] In addition, numerous private satellite television channels operate alongside the state networks.[^cite_note-arabmediasoc-366] Egypt is a major force in satellite broadcasting, being the first Arab country to launch its own satellite, [Nilesat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nilesat).&#x20;

### Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Egypt)



[Soad Hosny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soad_Hosny)



[Faten Hamama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faten_Hamama)



[Omar Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Sharif)



[Ahmed Zaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Zaki_\(actor\))

[Egyptian cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_cinema), the oldest in Africa and the Arab world, began in 1896 with film screenings in [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), [Cairo](6293.md), and [Port Said](205162.md). Early production started in 1907 with short documentaries, and the first feature films appeared in 1917, directed by [Mohammed Karim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Karim). By the 1920s and 1930s, the country's film scene expanded with productions such as [Layla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laila_\(1927_film\)) and [Sons of Aristocrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sons_of_Aristocrats), the first Egyptian talkie.[^cite_note-chamber-cinema-370] In 1936, [Studio Misr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Studio_Misr), financed by industrialist [Talaat Harb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talaat_Pasha_Harb), emerged as the leading Egyptian studio, a role the company retained for three decades.[^cite_note-371] For over 100 years, more than 4,000 films have been produced in Egypt, three quarters of the total Arab production.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373]&#x20;

The industry has been a dominant cultural force in the Arab world since its inception, earning the nickname "Arab Hollywood" and "Hollywood of the East".[^cite_note-meer-cinema-374][^cite_note-375][^cite_note-chamber-cinema-370] Its golden age, spanning the 1940s to the 1960s, saw Egypt become the world's third-largest film producer, with hundreds of films across genres achieving widespread regional popularity.[^cite_note-meer-cinema-374] Iconic filmmakers such as [Youssef Chahine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youssef_Chahine) and [Henry Barakat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Barakat), and celebrated actors including [Faten Hamama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faten_Hamama), helped establish Egyptian cinema as a major influence on Arab cultural identity. Today, Egyptian films continue to reach audiences across the Arab world and beyond, maintaining the country's historic role as a central hub for Arabic-language cinema.[^cite_note-meer-cinema-374]&#x20;

Egypt hosts several film festivals, which have become important platforms for both local and international filmmakers. The [Cairo International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo_International_Film_Festival), established in 1976, showcases a range of films from dramas to experimental works and attracts filmmakers and audiences from around the world.[^cite_note-film-festivals-376] It is listed by the [International Federation of Film Producers' Associations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIAPF) as one of the 11 competitive film festivals in the world.[^cite_note-377] The [Luxor African Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxor_African_Film_Festival) highlights African cinema, while the [El Gouna Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Gouna_Film_Festival) presents Arabic and international films. Other notable events include the Ismailia International Film Festival, which focuses on social and environmental themes, and the Alexandria Short Film Festival, which features narrative, documentary, and animated short films.[^cite_note-film-festivals-376]&#x20;

The number of cinemas increased with the emergence of [talking films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talking_film), and reached 395 in 1958. This number began to decline after the establishment of television in 1960 and the establishment of the public sector in cinemas in 1962. The cinema count fell to 297 in 1965, then to 141 in 1995, due to the circulation of films through video equipment though the boom of the film industry in this period. Due to laws and procedures that encouraged investment in the establishment of private cinemas, they increased again, especially in commercial centres. Their number reached 200 in 2001 and 400 in 2009.[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379]&#x20;

### Music

Main article: [Music of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Egypt)

Ancient Egyptians playing music

An ancient Egyptian mural of people playing music.

[Egyptian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Egypt) is a rich mixture of indigenous, Mediterranean, African and Western elements. It has been an integral part of [Egyptian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Egypt) since antiquity. The ancient [Egyptians](31912046.md) credited one of their gods [Hathor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hathor) with the invention of music, which [Osiris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osiris) in turn used as part of his effort to civilise the world. Egyptians used music instruments since then.[^cite_note-380]&#x20;

Contemporary Egyptian music has its roots in the work of early artists such as [Abdu al-Hamuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdu_al-Hamuli), Almaz, and Mahmoud Osman, whose contributions shaped and inspired later generations. It was recorded as early as the 1910s, with a thriving classical tradition developing in Cairo by the 1930s, blending indigenous folk influences with Western elements and producing iconic 20th-century stars such as [Sayed Darwish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayed_Darwish), [Umm Kulthum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Kulthum), [Mohammed Abdel Wahab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Abdel_Wahab), [Abdel Halim Hafez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel_Halim_Hafez), and [Baligh Hamdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baligh_Hamdi), many of whom became central to both popular culture and national identity.&#x20;



[Umm Kulthum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Kulthum), an icon of Egyptian music, often referred to as "Egypt's [Fourth Pyramid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giza_pyramid_complex)".

[^cite_note-381]

&#x20;In 2023, *[Rolling Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolling_Stone)* ranked Umm Kulthum at number 61 on its list of the 200 Greatest Singers of All Time.

[^cite_note-382]

[^cite_note-383]

The music scene evolved from the golden age of mid-20th century [modern Egyptian classical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Egypt#Modern_Egyptian_classical_and_pop_music) heavyweights, to the rise of [Egyptian pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_pop) in the 1980s–90s with artists such as [Amr Diab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amr_Diab), [Hisham Abbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hisham_Abbas), and [Mostafa Amar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mostafa_Amar).[^cite_note-aravanis-music-384] At the same time, [Omar Khairat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Khairat) emerged as a leading composer and pianist, blending [Western classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_classical_music) with distinctive Egyptian influences. He has written numerous acclaimed film scores and symphonic works which has made him one of Egypt's most internationally respected contemporary musicians.[^cite_note-385]&#x20;

In the 21st century, globalisation and the [2011 revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Egyptian_revolution) spurred diverse underground movements, with musicians and bands like [Ramy Essam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramy_Essam) and [Cairokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairokee) addressing identity and political themes.[^cite_note-aravanis-music-384] [Mahraganat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahraganat) emerged from working-class communities to become mainstream, developing out of [shaabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaabi) music, which has been part of Egyptian culture since the 1970s with pioneers such as [Ahmed Adaweya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Adaweya). Meanwhile, Egyptian [rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rap), [trap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trap_music), and [techno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Techno) gained prominence through artists like Abyusif, [Marwan Pablo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marwan_Pablo), and [Wegz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wegz).[^cite_note-aravanis-music-384]&#x20;

In recent years, Egypt's music scene has seen a generational shift, with new artists emerging as leading figures alongside established icons. According to [Spotify Wrapped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spotify_Wrapped) 2024, seven of the ten most-streamed songs in Egypt came from newer performers experimenting with [rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rap), [R\&B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%26B), [electronic fusions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_music), and [street music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underground_music) alongside traditional influences. Rising artists such as Eslam Kabonga, Shehab, [TUL8TE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TUL8TE), Mahmoud El Leithy, and Essam Sasa gained significant popularity, while established acts like Marwan Pablo, [Sherine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherine), [Cairokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairokee), and [Tamer Ashour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamer_Ashour) also remained highly streamed. The trend coincided with an 85 percent rise in local music consumption.[^cite_note-386] Egypt's music industry has seen rapid international growth through streaming platforms, with [Spotify](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spotify) reporting a fivefold rise in royalties since 2022, over 90 percent of earnings going to independent artists, and more than 80 percent of revenue coming from listeners outside Egypt.[^cite_note-387]&#x20;

### Dances

Egypt has a rich tradition of folk dances that reflect the country's regional and cultural diversity. In [Lower Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Egypt), dances such as *[ghawazi el sonbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghawazi)* (غوازي سنباط), *raqs el hagala* (رقص الحجلة), and *welad el sayala* (أولاد السيالة) incorporate colourful costumes and props to tell a story. For example, *welad el sayala*, performed in [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), uses pocket knives and chairs to represent the lives and celebrations of fishermen and their communities.[^cite_note-nadine-khaled-388] In [Upper Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Egypt), dances include stick dances such as *raqs el assaya* (رقص العصا) and *[tahtib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahtib)* (تحطيب), which are traditionally performed by men at weddings and festive occasions. These dances often symbolise strength and skill, with tahtib in particular originating from ancient martial arts traditions.[^cite_note-nadine-khaled-388] Nubian dance, originating in [Nubian communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nubians) in Egypt's south, are characterised by rhythmic arm movements and upbeat tempos, often accompanied by the tambourine duff.[^cite_note-nadine-khaled-388]&#x20;



A [tanoura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanoura_\(dance\)) dancer performing

Egypt is often considered the home of [belly dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belly_dance). Egyptian belly dance has two main styles: *[raqs baladi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raqs_baladi)* (رقص بلدي) and *[raqs sharqi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raqs_sharqi)* (رقص شرقي). There are also numerous folkloric and character dances that may be part of an Egyptian-style belly dancer's repertoire, as well as the modern *shaabi* dance (رقص شعبي), which shares some elements with raqs baladi. Belly dancing emphasises fluid movements of the hips, belly, and arms, often performed to the rhythm of the tabla drum, and is closely associated with femininity and performance in cinema and theatre.[^cite_note-nadine-khaled-388]&#x20;

Other prominent forms of Egyptian dance include Sufi *[tanoura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanoura_\(dance\))* (رقص التنورة) dancing, which involves multilayered skirts and is performed both as a spiritual practice to achieve trance-like states and as a cultural performance at festivals and tourist sites.[^cite_note-nadine-khaled-388]&#x20;

### Museums

Main article: [List of museums in Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Egypt)



The [Egyptian Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Museum) in Cairo

Egypt is home to one of the world's oldest civilisations. It has engaged with numerous cultures and nations throughout its history and has experienced a vast array of eras, from the prehistoric age to modern times, encompassing periods such as [ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt), [Ptolemaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_Kingdom), [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Egypt), [the Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_in_the_Middle_Ages), [Ottoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Egypt), and the [Alawiyya dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Egypt_under_the_Muhammad_Ali_dynasty).&#x20;



Tutankhamun's burial mask is one of the major attractions of the [Egyptian Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Museum).

Notable museums in Egypt include the [Egyptian Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Museum) in Cairo, which houses over 120,000 items and is one of the world's largest museums as well as the first national museum in the Middle East, opened in 1902;[^cite_note-389] the [National Museum of Egyptian Civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Egyptian_Civilization), home to 50,000 artefacts from various eras and the resting place of 22 ancient Egyptian kings and queens relocated there in 2021 during the [Pharaohs' Golden Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharaohs%27_Golden_Parade); and the [Abdeen Palace Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdeen_Palace_Museum), one of Egypt's most famous royal palaces, which contains five museums showcasing arms, royal belongings, silverware, historical documents, and presidential gifts.[^cite_note-390]&#x20;

The [Grand Egyptian Museum](5359115.md) is a museum housing the largest collection of ancient Egyptian artefacts in the world.[^cite_note-391] It is the largest museum in the world dedicated to a single civilisation.[^cite_note-Britannica-392][^cite_note-393] The museum is sited on 50 hectares (120 acres) of land approximately two kilometres (1.2 miles) from the Giza Necropolis and is part of a new master plan for the plateau.[^cite_note-394] It features a six-storey atrium with the 82-ton [Statue of Ramesses II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statue_of_Ramesses_II) and a grand staircase, while its galleries display artefacts spanning ancient Egypt's history.[^cite_note-\:3-395] The Tutankhamun exhibition showcases 5,600 objects from his tomb, including his gold mask and royal regalia. It is arranged around three central themes, life, death, and the afterlife, and features two opposing pathways: a chronological journey through his life and reign, and a forensic exploration of his tomb and excavation.[^cite_note-\:0-4] The museum opened on 1 November 2025.[^cite_note-396]&#x20;

### Festivals



Moulid celebrations in [Muizz Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muizz_Street), Cairo

Egypt observes several religious Muslim and Christian festivals as public holidays. These include [Coptic Christmas](6237.md), [Eid al-Fitr](154944.md), [Eid al-Adha](358226.md), [Islamic New Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_New_Year), the [Day of Arafah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Day_of_Arafah), and [Moulid al-Nabawi](1285650.md).[^cite_note-397]&#x20;

*[Sham Ennessim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sham_Ennessim)* (شم النسيم) is an annual [festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festival) in Egypt marking the beginning of spring, celebrated by Egyptians of all religions and recognised as an official [public holiday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holiday).[^cite_note-asante-398] It is observed on [Easter Monday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Monday), the day after [Easter](9325.md),[^cite_note-\:3-shamenessim-399] typically between the [Egyptian months](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_calendar) of [Paremoude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paremoude) (April) and [Pashons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashons) (May). The festival is traditionally celebrated outdoors, with picnics in public gardens, along the [Nile](21244.md), or at the zoo. Typical foods include *[fesikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fesikh)*, lettuce, [green onions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scallion), and [lupin beans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lupin_bean). Boiled eggs are often coloured and eaten or exchanged as gifts.[^cite_note-asante-398] It is generally held that Sham Ennessim is a continuation of early forms of springtime festivities dating back over 4,500 years.[^cite_note-asante-398]&#x20;

[Moulids](1285650.md#Other_uses), or saint's festivals, are a long-standing tradition throughout Egypt, celebrating both Islamic and Christian saints. The majority are Islamic and typically feature [dhikr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhikr), the ritual chanting and recitation of prayers, as well as performances by Sufi groups, including music and spiritual gatherings.[^cite_note-googlearts-moulid-400] Christian moulids follow comparable festive patterns. These events blend religious devotion with communal celebration, often centring on a shrine or mosque where pilgrims seek blessings through prayer, recitation of religious texts, circumambulation, and other acts of veneration.[^cite_note-googlearts-moulid-400] They are also social and cultural occasions, with food, games, rides, and temporary stalls set up in the surrounding streets.[^cite_note-googlearts-moulid-400] Major festivals, such as the *[Moulid Abu El Haggag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Haggag_Mosque#Mawlid_celebration)* (مولد أبو الحجاج) in [Luxor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxor), attract thousands of participants. It features boats and shrines paraded through the city in his honour, a tradition with origins dating back to [ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt).[^cite_note-googlearts-moulid-400] The procession closely resembles the rituals of the [Opet Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opet_Festival).[^cite_note-401]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Egyptian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_cuisine)



[Koshary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koshary), one of Egypt's national dishes

[Egyptian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_cuisine) draws on the agricultural traditions of the [Nile Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile_Valley) and [Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile_Delta), and makes heavy use of poultry, legumes, vegetables, and fruits. Staple dishes include *[mahshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolma)*, rice-stuffed vegetables and grape leaves, *[falafel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falafel)*, mainly known locally as *ta'ameya*, *[shawarma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawarma)*, *[kabab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebab#Egypt)*, and *[kofta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koftet_el_hati)*. Traditional Egyptian specialities include *[ful medames](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ful_medames)*, mashed fava beans; *[feteer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feteer)*, a flaky, layered pie with various fillings;[^cite_note-Marzouk-402] *[hawawshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawawshi)*, spiced minced meat baked inside bread;[^cite_note-403] *[molokhiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molokhiya)*, a jute leaf stew; and *[koshary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koshary)*, a mix of lentils, rice, and pasta, which is recognised as [intangible cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intangible_cultural_heritage) by [UNESCO](21786641.md).[^cite_note-404] Many Egyptian dishes are traditionally prepared hands-on at home, often slow-cooked, based on the culinary practices of rural kitchens passed down through generations,[^cite_note-405] including stews such as *[bamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamia)*, okra stew; *[qolqas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qolqas)*, taro root stew; and baked dishes, such as *[rozz me'ammar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rozz_me%27ammar)*, a baked rice dish made with milk, butter, and [eshta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qeshta); as well as *[macarona bil-bechamel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macarona_bil-bechamel)*, a pasta bake with [penne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penne), spiced meat sauce, and [béchamel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bechamel_sauce).&#x20;

Bread holds a central place in Egyptian cuisine and dining traditions, with more than 60 different types found across the country.[^cite_note-rawigivinglife-406] *[Eish baladi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eish_baladi)*, a round, whole-wheat flatbread, is a staple of nearly every meal and is commonly used as an edible utensil for dipping and scooping rather than simply accompanying dishes.[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-Basan2007-408] Cheesemaking in Egypt dates back to the [First Dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Dynasty_of_Egypt),[^cite_note-LucasHarris2012-409] with *[Domiati cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domiati_cheese)* being the most widely consumed today.[^cite_note-410][^cite_note-RobinsonTamime1996-411] Meat plays an important role in Egyptian cuisine, with poultry such as [squab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squab),[^cite_note-newly_translated_cookbook-412] chicken, duck, [quail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quail), and goose being common, alongside lamb and beef for grilling and stews.[^cite_note-413] Cured meats such as *[bastirma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bastirma)* and *[sogoq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sogoq)* are traditionally consumed. Egyptian *bastirma* is made from salted, spiced, and air-dried beef or water buffalo,[^cite_note-gagaoua-414][^cite_note-415] and *sogoq*, a spicy sausage of ground beef stuffed into thin casings and left to dry and ferment.[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417] Fish, especially [tilapia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tilapia) and [mullet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mullet_\(fish\)), are widely consumed, with seafood in general being particularly prevalent in coastal cities like [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), [Suez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez), and [Port Said](205162.md). A large portion of Egyptian cuisine is vegetarian due to limited grazing land, historical agricultural practices, and the religious customs of [Coptic Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_Christians), who periodically observe a [vegan diet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lent).[^cite_note-418]&#x20;

The cuisine commonly features an assortment of spices and aromatics such as [cumin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumin), [coriander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coriander), [cardamom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardamom), [chilli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chili_pepper), [aniseed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aniseed), [bay leaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_leaves), [dill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dill), [parsley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsley), [garlic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garlic), [ginger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ginger), [cinnamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinnamon), [mint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamiaceae), and [cloves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloves), with cumin being the most commonly used.[^cite_note-419] Popular desserts include [baqlawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baqlawa), [basbousa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basbousa), [kunafa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunafa), and [qatayef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatayef), often featuring dates, honey, syrup, nuts and [semolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semolina). Tea is the national drink, while coffee is also common and is traditionally prepared in the [Turkish style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_coffee). Other popular beverages in Egypt include *[karkadeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karkadeh)*, hibiscus tea; *['asir asab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarcane_juice)*, sugarcane juice; *'[erq sous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erq_sous)*, liquorice juice; *[kharob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharob)*, carob juice; *[amar eddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qamar_al-din)*, apricot drink; and *[sobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sobia)*, sweet coconut milk drink. [Beer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_in_Egypt) is the most popular alcoholic beverage,[^cite_note-420] including traditional types like *[bouza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bouza_\(beer\))*, made from [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley) and bread and consumed since the [Predynastic era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Egypt).[^cite_note-421]&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Egypt)



A crowd at Cairo Stadium watching the [Egypt national football team](1022953.md)

[Football](10568.md) is the most popular [national sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_sport) of Egypt. The [Cairo Derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo_Derby) is one of the fiercest derbies in Africa, and the BBC picked it as one of the 7 toughest derbies in the world.[^cite_note-422] [Al Ahly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Ahly_SC) is the most successful club of the 20th century in the African continent according to CAF, closely followed by their rivals [Zamalek SC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zamalek_SC). They are known as the "[African Club of the Century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CAF_Clubs_of_the_20th_Century)". With twenty titles, Al Ahly is currently the world's most successful club in terms of international trophies, surpassing Italy's [A.C. Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A.C._Milan) and Argentina's [Boca Juniors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boca_Juniors), both having eighteen.[^cite_note-423]&#x20;

The [Egyptian national football team](1022953.md), known as the Pharaohs, won the [African Cup of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Cup_of_Nations) seven times, including three times in a row in 2006, 2008, and 2010. Considered the most successful African national team and one which has reached the top 10 of the FIFA world rankings,[^cite_note-424] Egypt has qualified for the [FIFA World Cup](11370.md) four times.[^cite_note-425] Two goals from star player [Mohamed Salah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Salah) in their last qualifying game took Egypt through to the [2018 FIFA World Cup](13327177.md).[^cite_note-426] The Egyptian youth national team Young Pharaohs won the bronze medal of the [2001 FIFA youth world cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_FIFA_World_Youth_Championship) in Argentina. Egypt was 4th place in the football tournament in the [1928](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1928_Summer_Olympics) and the [1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Summer_Olympics)[^cite_note-427] Olympics.[^cite_note-428]&#x20;

[Squash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squash_\(sport\)) and tennis are other popular sports in Egypt. The Egyptian squash team has been competitive in international championships since the 1930s. [Amr Shabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amr_Shabana), [Ali Farag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Farag) and [Ramy Ashour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramy_Ashour) are Egypt's best players and all were ranked the world's number one squash player. Egypt has won the Squash World Championships five times, with the last title being in [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Men%27s_World_Team_Squash_Championships).&#x20;

Egypt [hosted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_World_Men%27s_Handball_Championship) the [IHF World Men's Handball Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IHF_World_Men%27s_Handball_Championship) in 1999 and hosted it again in [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_World_Men%27s_Handball_Championship). In 2001, the [national handball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_men%27s_national_handball_team) achieved its best result in the tournament by reaching fourth place. Egypt has won the [African Men's Handball Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Men%27s_Handball_Championship) five times, being the best team in Africa. Egypt won the [Mediterranean Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_at_the_2013_Mediterranean_Games) in [2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handball_at_the_2013_Mediterranean_Games), the [Beach Handball World Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beach_Handball_World_Championships) in [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Beach_Handball_World_Championships) and the [Summer Youth Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_at_the_2010_Summer_Youth_Olympics) in [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handball_at_the_2010_Summer_Youth_Olympics_%E2%80%93_Boys%27_tournament). Among all African nations, the [Egyptian national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_national_basketball_team) holds the record for best performance at the [Basketball World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_Basketball_World_Cup) and at the [Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketball_at_the_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430] Further, the team has won a record number of 16 medals at the [African Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_Africa_Championship).&#x20;

[Egypt has taken part](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_at_the_Olympics) in the [Summer Olympic Games](27566.md) since 1912 and has hosted [several other international competitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:International_sports_competitions_hosted_by_Egypt), including [the first Mediterranean Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Mediterranean_Games) in 1951, the [1991 All-Africa Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_All-Africa_Games), the [2009 FIFA U-20 World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_FIFA_U-20_World_Cup) and the [1953](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Pan_Arab_Games), [1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1965_Pan_Arab_Games) and [2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Pan_Arab_Games) editions of the [Pan Arab Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_Arab_Games).&#x20;

[^cite_note-lang-3]: &#x20;[Literary Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Standard_Arabic) is the sole official language.<sup>[\[2\]](#cite_note-Provisional_Constitution-2)</sup> [Egyptian Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Arabic) is the [spoken language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spoken_language). Other [dialects and minority languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Egypt) are spoken regionally.

[^cite_note-relig-7]: &#x20;While [Islam](6037917.md) is the majority and official religion of the country, the size of the country's historic [Christian minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Egypt) is highly controversial and disputed by various entities and groups. Estimates range from as low as 5% to as high as 20%. Since 2006, religion has been omitted from censuses after widespread claims that the figures had been distorted.<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-\:0-4)</sup><sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-churches-deleg-5)</sup><sup>[\[5\]](#cite_note-6)</sup>

[^cite_note-51]: &#x20;The ending of the Hebrew form is either a [dual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_\(grammatical_number\)) or an ending identical to the dual in form (perhaps a [locative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Locative)), and this has sometimes been taken as referring to the two kingdoms of Upper and Lower Egypt. However, the application of the (possibly) "dual" ending to some toponyms and other words, a development peculiar to Hebrew, does not in fact imply any "two-ness" about the place. The ending is found, for example, in the Hebrew words for such single entities as 'water' (מַיִם), 'noon' (צָהֳרַיִם), 'sky/heaven' (שָׁמַיִם), and in the *[qere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qere_and_Ketiv)* – but not the original *ketiv* – of 'Jerusalem' (ירושל\[י]ם). It should also be noted that the dual ending – which may or may not be what the *-áyim* in *Mitzráyim* actually represents – was available to other Semitic languages, such as Arabic, but was not applied to Egypt. See *inter alia* Aaron Demsky ("Hebrew Names in the Dual Form and the Toponym Yerushalayim" in Demsky (ed.) *These Are the Names: Studies in Jewish Onomastics*, Vol. 3 (Ramat Gan, 2002), pp. 11–20), [Page 128](https://books.google.com/books?id=p1AMBAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA128) in Hurvitz, Avi (2014). "List of Abbreviations and Sigla". *A Concise Lexicon of Late Biblical Hebrew*. pp. 18–243. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1163/9789004266438_004](https://doi.org/10.1163%2F9789004266438_004). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-90-04-26643-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-90-04-26643-8). and Na'aman, Nadav (31 December 2008). "Shaaraim – The Gateway To The Kingdom Of Judah". *The Journal of Hebrew Scriptures*. **8**. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.5508/jhs.2008.v8.a24](https://doi.org/10.5508%2Fjhs.2008.v8.a24).
